On the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann Limit of the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system
Ning Jiang, Yuanjie Lei, Huijiang Zhao

Abstract
. For the whole range of cutoff intermolecular interactions, we give a rigorous mathematical justification of the limit from the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system to the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system as the light speed tends to infinity. Such a limit is shown to hold global-in-time in the perturbative framework. The key point in our analysis is to deduce certain a priori estimates which are independent of the light speed and the main difficulty is due to the degeneracy of the dissipative effect of the electromagnetic field for large light speed.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
The problem

The motion of dilute ionized plasmas consisting of two-species particles (e.g., electrons and ions) under the influence of binary collisions and the self-consistent electromagnetic field can be modelled by the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system (cf.
[
3
, Chapter 19]
as well as
[
21
, Chapter 6.6]
)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F^{c}_{+}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F^{c}_{+}+\frac{e_{+}}{m_{+}}\left(E^{c}+\frac{v}{c}\times B^{c}\right)\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{c}_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q\left(F^{c}_{+},F^{c}_{+}\right)+Q\left(F^{c}_{+},F^{c}_{-}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F^{c}_{-}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F^{c}_{-}-\frac{e_{-}}{m_{-}}\left(E^{c}+\frac{v}{c}\times B^{c}\right)\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{c}_{-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q\left(F^{c}_{-},F^{c}_{+}\right)+Q\left(F^{c}_{-},F^{c}_{-}\right).

(1.1)

Here the electromagnetic field
\left[E^{c},B^{c}\right]=\left[E^{c}(t,x),B^{c}(t,x)\right]
satisfies the Maxwell equations

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}E^{c}-\nabla_{x}\times B^{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{4\pi}{c}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}v\left(e_{+}F^{c}_{+}-e_{-}F^{c}_{-}\right)dv,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\partial_{t}B^{c}+\nabla_{x}\times E^{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(1.2)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\pi{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\left(e_{+}F^{c}_{+}-e_{-}F^{c}_{-}\right)dv,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot B^{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

where
\nabla_{x}=\left[\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}},\partial_{x_{3}}\right],\nabla_{v}=\left[\partial_{v_{1}},\partial_{v_{2}},\partial_{v_{3}}\right]
. The unknown functions
F^{c}_{\pm}=F^{c}_{\pm}(t,x,v)\geq 0
are the number density functions for the ions (
+
) and electrons (
-
) with position
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and velocity
v=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
at time
t\geq 0
, respectively,
e_{\pm}
and
m_{\pm}
the magnitudes of their charges and masses, and
c
the light speed.

Let
F(v)
,
G(v)
be two number density functions for two types of particles with masses
m_{\pm}
and diameters
\sigma_{\pm}
, then the collision operator
Q(F,G)(v)
for cutoff intermolecular interactions is defined as (cf.
[
3
]
)

\displaystyle Q(F,G)(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\sigma_{+}+\sigma_{-})^{2}}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|u-v|^{\gamma}{\bf b}\left(\frac{\omega\cdot(v-u)}{|u-v|}\right)\left\{F(v^{\prime})G(u^{\prime})-F(v)G(u)\right\}d\omega du

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle Q_{gain}(F,G)-Q_{loss}(F,G).

Here
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
and
{\bf b}
, the angular part of the collision kernel, satisfies Grad’s cutoff assumption (cf.
[
14
]
)

0\leq{\bf b}(\cos\theta)\leq C|\cos\theta|

(1.3)

for some positive constant
C>0
. The deviation angle
\pi-2\theta
satisfies
\cos\theta=\omega\cdot(v-u)/{|v-u|}
.
Moreover, for
m_{1},m_{2}\in\{m_{+},m_{-}\}
,

v^{\prime}=v-\frac{2m_{2}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}[(v-u)\cdot\omega]\omega,\quad u^{\prime}=u+\frac{2m_{1}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}[(v-u)\cdot\omega]\omega,

which denote velocities
(v^{\prime},u^{\prime})
after a collision of particles having velocities
(v,u)
before the collision and vice versa. Notice that the above identities follow from the conservation of momentum
m_{1}v+m_{2}u
and energy
\frac{1}{2}m_{1}|v|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}m_{2}|u|^{2}
.

The exponent
\gamma\in(-3,1]
in the kinetic part of the collision kernel is determined by the potential of intermolecular force, which is classified into the soft potential case for
-3<\gamma<0
, the Maxwell molecular case for
\gamma=0
, and the hard potential case for
0<\gamma\leq 1
which includes the hard sphere model with
\gamma=1
and
{\bf b}(\cos\theta)=C|\cos\theta|
for some positive constant
C>0
. For the soft potentials, the case
-2\leq\gamma<0
is called the moderately soft potentials while
-3<\gamma<-2
is called the very soft potentials, cf.
[
34
]
.

For the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system (
1.1
), (
1.2
), if one let the light speed
c\to+\infty
, one can then formally deduce that the solution
\left[F^{c}_{+}(t,x,v),F^{c}_{-}(t,x,v),E^{c}(t,x),B^{c}(t,x)\right]
of the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system (
1.1
), (
1.2
) converges to
\left[F^{\infty}_{+}(t,x,v),F^{\infty}_{-}(t,x,v),E^{\infty}(t,x),B^{\infty}(t,x)\right]
with
E^{\infty}(t,x)

\equiv\nabla_{x}\phi^{\infty}(t,x)
for some scalar function
\phi^{\infty}(t,x)
, and
\left[F^{\infty}_{+}(t,x,v),F^{\infty}_{-}(t,x,v),\phi^{\infty}(t,x)\right]
solves the following Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F^{\infty}_{+}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F^{\infty}_{+}+\frac{e_{+}}{m_{+}}\nabla_{x}\phi^{\infty}\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{\infty}_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q\left(F^{\infty}_{+},F^{\infty}_{+}\right)+Q\left(F^{\infty}_{+},F^{\infty}_{-}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F^{\infty}_{-}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F^{\infty}_{-}-\frac{e_{-}}{m_{-}}\nabla_{x}\phi^{\infty}\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{\infty}_{-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q\left(F^{\infty}_{-},F^{\infty}_{+}\right)+Q\left(F^{\infty}_{-},F^{\infty}_{-}\right),

(1.4)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\phi^{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\pi{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\left(e_{+}F^{\infty}_{+}-e_{-}F^{\infty}_{-}\right)dv.

It is easy to see that (
1.1
)
3
is equivalent to the fact that
E^{\infty}(t,x)
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E^{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}v\left(e_{+}F^{\infty}_{+}-e_{-}F^{\infty}_{-}\right)dv,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\times E^{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(1.5)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(e_{+}F^{\infty}_{+}-e_{-}F^{\infty}_{-}\right)dv.

If we define

\displaystyle\mu_{+}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{0}}{e_{+}}\left(\frac{m_{+}}{2\pi\kappa_{B}T_{0}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{m_{+}|v|^{2}}{2\kappa_{B}T_{0}}\right),

\displaystyle\mu_{-}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{0}}{e_{-}}\left(\frac{m_{-}}{2\pi\kappa_{B}T_{0}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{m_{-}|v|^{2}}{2\kappa_{B}T_{0}}\right),

(1.6)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\mathfrak{E}_{1}(x),\mathfrak{E}_{2}(x),\mathfrak{E}_{3}(x)\right],

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\mathfrak{B}_{1}(x),\mathfrak{B}_{2}(x),\mathfrak{B}_{3}(x)\right],

where
\kappa_{B}>0
is the Boltzmann constant,
n_{0}>0
and
T_{0}>0
are constant
reference number density and temperature, respectively, and the reference bulk
velocities have been chosen to be zero, then it is easy to check that
[\mu_{+}(v),\mu_{-}(v),\mathfrak{E}(x),\mathfrak{B}(x)]
is a solution to the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system (
1.1
), (
1.2
) iff

\mathfrak{E}(x)=0,\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot\mathfrak{B}(x)=0,\quad\nabla_{x}\times\mathfrak{B}(x)=0

hold for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, from which, the fact that
\mathfrak{B}(x)
is always assumed to be bounded, and Liouville’s theorem, one can deduce that

\mathfrak{B}(x)\equiv\mathfrak{B}=\left[\mathfrak{B}_{1},\mathfrak{B}_{2},\mathfrak{B}_{3}\right]
, where
\left[\mathfrak{B}_{1},\mathfrak{B}_{2},\mathfrak{B}_{3}\right]
is any constant vector.

The main purpose of this paper is to give a rigorous mathematical justification of the above formal limit, which is global-in-time, in the perturbative framework, i.e. in the regime when the solution
\left[F^{c}_{+}(t,x,v),\right.

\left.F^{c}_{-}(t,x,v),E^{c}(t,x),B^{c}(t,x)\right]
of the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system (
1.1
), (
1.2
) and the solution
\left[F^{\infty}_{+}(t,x,v),\right.

\left.F^{\infty}_{-}(t,x,v),\phi^{\infty}(t,x)\right]
of the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system (
1.1
) are a small perturbation of the equilibrium states
[\mu_{+}(v),\mu_{-}(v),0,\mathfrak{B}]
and
[\mu_{+}(v),\mu_{-}(v),0]
, respectively. Here and in the rest of this paper,
\mathfrak{B}=\left[\mathfrak{B}_{1},\mathfrak{B}_{2},\mathfrak{B}_{3}\right]
is any given constant vector.

To this end, we focus on the Cauchy problem of the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system (
1.1
), (
1.2
) with prescribed initial data

F^{c}_{\pm}(0,x,v)=F^{c}_{0,\pm}(v,x),\quad E^{c}(0,x)=E^{c}_{0}(x),\quad B^{c}(0,x)=B^{c}_{0}(x),

(1.7)

which satisfy the compatibility conditions

\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{c}_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(F^{c}_{0,+}-F^{c}_{0,-}\right)dv,\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot B^{c}_{0}=0,

(1.8)

and the Cauchy problem of the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system (
1.1
) with initial data

F^{\infty}_{\pm}(0,x,v)=F^{\infty}_{0,\pm}(x,v).

(1.9)

Without loss of generality, we assume in the rest of this paper that all the physical constants such as
m_{\pm},e_{\pm},\sigma_{\pm}
, and the generic constant
4\pi
except the light speed
c
are chosen to be one. Under such an assumption, we can normalize the above Maxwellians accordingly as

\mu(v)=\mu_{+}(v)=\mu_{-}(v)=(2\pi)^{-\frac{3}{2}}e^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2}}.

Moreover, if we set
\epsilon=\frac{1}{c}
, then we will use
F^{\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),B^{\epsilon}(t,x),F^{\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v),E_{0}^{\epsilon}(x),B_{0}^{\epsilon}(x)
to denote
F^{c}_{\pm}(t,x,v),

E^{c}(t,x),B^{c}(t,x),F^{c}_{0,\pm}(x,v),E^{c}_{0}(x),B^{c}_{0}(x)
, respectively, in the rest of this paper.

1.2.
Notations

To continue, we first introduce some notations used throughout the paper.

•

A\lesssim B
means that there is a generic constant
C>0
, which is independent of
\epsilon
, such that
A\leq CB
.
A\sim B
means
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
;

•

The multi-indices
\alpha=[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}]
and
\beta=[\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}]
will be used to record spatial and velocity derivatives, respectively. And
\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}=\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{x_{1}}\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{x_{2}}\partial^{\alpha_{3}}_{x_{3}}\partial^{\beta_{1}}_{v_{1}}\partial^{\beta_{2}}_{v_{2}}\partial^{\beta_{3}}_{v_{3}}
. Similarly, the notation
\partial^{\alpha}
will be used when
\beta=0
and likewise for
\partial_{\beta}
. The length of
\alpha
is denoted by
|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}
.
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
means that no component of
\alpha^{\prime}
is greater than the corresponding component of
\alpha
, and
\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha
means that
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
and
|\alpha^{\prime}|<|\alpha|
. And it is convenient to write
\partial_{i}=\partial_{x_{i}}
for
i=1,2,3
and
\left|\nabla_{x}^{k}u\right|=\sum\limits_{|\alpha|=k}\left|\partial^{\alpha}u\right|
;

•

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is used to denote the
{L^{2}_{v}}\times L^{2}_{v}
inner product in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}
, with the
{L^{2}}
norm
|\cdot|_{L^{2}}
. For notational simplicity,
(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the
{L^{2}}\times L^{2}
inner product either in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{x}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v}
or in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{x}
with the
{L^{2}}\times L^{2}
norm
\|\cdot\|
. Moreover
|f|_{\nu}\equiv\left|\sqrt{\nu}f\right|_{L^{2}},\|f\|_{\nu}\equiv\left\|\sqrt{\nu}f\right\|
, where
\nu(v)
is the collision frequency defined by (
2.11
) satisfying
\nu(v)\sim\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}
,
\langle v\rangle=\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}
;

•

For a complex-valued function
g(t,x,v)
,
\mathfrak{R}g
and
\overline{g}
denote the real part of
g(t,x,v)
and the complex conjugate of
g(t,x,v)
, respectively. Moreover,
\langle[v,-v],[f,g]\rangle=\langle v,f-g\rangle,

([v,-v],[f,g])=(v,f-g),

(f\ |\ g)=(f,\overline{g}),\langle f\ |\ g\rangle=\langle f,\overline{g}\rangle
;

•

\|f(t,\cdot,\cdot)\|_{L^{p}_{x}L^{q}_{v}}=\left({\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}}}\left({\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}}|f(t,x,v)|^{q}dv\right)^{\frac{p}{q}}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
;

•

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left(\Lambda^{s}g\right)(t,x,v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\xi|^{s}\hat{g}(t,\xi,v)e^{2\pi ix\cdot\xi}d\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\xi|^{s}\mathcal{F}[g](t,\xi,v)e^{2\pi ix\cdot\xi}d\xi

with
\hat{g}(t,\xi,v)\equiv\mathcal{F}[g](t,\xi,v)
being the Fourier transform of
g(t,x,v)
with respect to
x
. The homogeneous Sobolev space
\dot{H}^{s}\times\dot{H}^{s}
is the Banach space consisting of all
g
satisfying
\|g\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}<+\infty
, where

\|g(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\equiv\left\|\left(\Lambda^{s}g\right)(t,x,v)\right\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}=\left\||\xi|^{s}\hat{g}(t,\xi,v)\right\|_{L^{2}_{\xi,v}}.

2.
Main results and ideas

In this section, we will state our main results and sketch our main ideas to prove them. To this end, we first give our main results in section 2.1.

2.1.
Main results

To make the presentation clear, we divided this section into two parts, the first part is concentrated on the hard sphere model.

2.1.1.
Main result for the hard sphere model

In the perturbative framework, we can further define the perturbation
f^{\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v)
by

\displaystyle F^{\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu(v)+\mu^{1/2}(v)f^{\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v)\geq 0,\ \ B^{\epsilon}(t,x)=\mathfrak{B}+\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t,x)

(2.1)

with initial data

\displaystyle F^{\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v)=\mu(v)+\mu^{1/2}(v)f^{\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v),\quad\quad B^{\epsilon}_{0}(x)=\mathfrak{B}+\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}_{0}(x)

(2.2)

respectively, then we can deduce from (
2.1
) that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f^{\epsilon}_{\pm}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f^{\epsilon}_{\pm}\mp E^{\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}\mp\frac{1}{2}E^{\epsilon}\cdot vf^{\epsilon}_{\pm}\pm E^{\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{\epsilon}_{\pm}

(2.3)

\displaystyle\pm\epsilon\left\{v\times\left(\mathfrak{B}+\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}\right)\right\}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{\epsilon}_{\pm}+{L}_{\pm}f^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\Gamma}_{\pm}\left(f^{\epsilon},f^{\epsilon}\right),

\displaystyle\epsilon\partial_{t}E^{\epsilon}-\nabla_{x}\times\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\epsilon{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}v\mu^{1/2}(v)\left(f^{\epsilon}_{+}-f^{\epsilon}_{-}\right)dv,

\displaystyle\epsilon\partial_{t}\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}+\nabla_{x}\times E^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\mu^{1/2}(v)\left(f^{\epsilon}_{+}-f^{\epsilon}_{-}\right)dv,\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}=0

with prescribed initial data

f^{\epsilon}_{\pm}(0,x,v)=f^{\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v),\quad E^{\epsilon}(0,x)=E^{\epsilon}_{0}(x),\quad\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(0,x)=\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}_{0}(x),

(2.4)

which satisfy the compatibility conditions

\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{\epsilon}_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mu^{1/2}(v)\left(f^{\epsilon}_{0,+}-f^{\epsilon}_{0,-}\right)dv,\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}_{0}=0.

(2.5)

For later use, setting
g=\left[g_{+},g_{-}\right]
, the first equation of
(\ref{f})
can be also rewritten as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f^{\epsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f^{\epsilon}-E^{\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}q_{1}

(2.6)

\displaystyle+q_{0}E^{\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{\epsilon}+q_{0}\epsilon\left\{v\times\left(\mathfrak{B}+\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}\right)\right\}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{\epsilon}+{L}f^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}q_{0}E^{\epsilon}\cdot vf^{\epsilon}+{\Gamma}\left(f^{\epsilon},f^{\epsilon}\right),

where

q_{0}={\textrm{diag}}(1,-1),\quad q_{1}=[1,-1],

(2.7)

and the linearized collision operator
L=[L_{+},L_{-}]
together with the nonlinear collision operator
\Gamma=[\Gamma_{+},\Gamma_{-}]
are respectively defined by

Lg=[L_{+}g,L_{-}g],\quad\quad\quad\Gamma(g,h)=[\Gamma_{+}(g,h),\Gamma_{-}(g,h)]

(2.8)

with

\displaystyle{L}_{\pm}g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\bf\mu}^{-1/2}\left\{{Q\left(\mu,{\bf\mu}^{1/2}(g_{\pm}+g_{\mp})\right)+2Q\left(\mu^{1/2}g_{\pm},\mu\right)}\right\},

\displaystyle{\Gamma}_{\pm}(g,h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf\mu}^{-1/2}\left\{Q\left({\bf\mu}^{1/2}g_{\pm},{\bf\mu}^{1/2}h_{\pm}\right)+Q\left({\bf\mu}^{1/2}g_{\pm},{\bf\mu}^{1/2}h_{\mp}\right)\right\}.

For the linearized collision operator
L
, it is well known (cf.
[
17
]
) that it is non-negative and the null space
\mathcal{N}
of
{L}
is spanned by

{\mathcal{N}}={\textrm{span}}\left\{[1,0]\mu^{1/2},[0,1]\mu^{1/2},[v_{i},v_{i}]{\mu}^{1/2}(1\leq i\leq 3),[|v|^{2},|v|^{2}]{\bf\mu}^{1/2}\right\}.

Let
{\bf P}
be the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v})\times L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v})
to
\mathcal{N}
, then for any given function
g(t,x,v)\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v})\times L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}_{v})
, one has

{\bf P}g={a^{g}_{+}(t,x)[1,0]\mu^{1/2}+a^{g}_{-}(t,x)[0,1]\mu^{1/2}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}b^{g}_{i}(t,x)[1,1]v_{i}{\mu}^{1/2}+c^{g}(t,x)[1,1]\left(|v|^{2}-3\right)}{\bf\mu}^{1/2}

(2.9)

with

\displaystyle a^{g}_{\pm}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}{\bf\mu}^{1/2}(v)g_{\pm}(t,x,v)dv,

\displaystyle b^{g}_{i}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}v_{i}{\bf\mu}^{1/2}(v)\left(g_{+}(t,x,v)+g_{-}(t,x,v)\right)dv,

\displaystyle c^{g}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{12}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}\left(|v|^{2}-3\right){\bf\mu}^{1/2}(v)\left(g_{+}(t,x,v)+g_{-}(t,x,v)\right)dv.

The expression (
2.9
) can be rewritten as
{\bf P}g=[{\bf P}g_{+},{\bf P}g_{-}]
with

{\bf P}_{\pm}g={a^{g}_{\pm}(t,x)\mu^{1/2}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}b^{g}_{i}(t,x)v_{i}{\mu}^{1/2}+c^{g}(t,x)\left(|v|^{2}-3\right)}{\bf\mu}^{1/2}.

Therefore, we have the following macro-micro decomposition with respect to the given global Maxwellian
\mu(v)
, cf.
[
18
,
28
]

g(t,x,v)={\bf P}g(t,x,v)+\{{\bf I}-{\bf P}\}g(t,x,v),

(2.10)

where
{\bf I}
denotes the identity operator, and
{\bf P}g
and
\{{\bf I}-{\bf P}\}g
are called the macroscopic and the microscopic component of
g(t,x,v)
, respectively.
Moreover, it is easy to see that
L
can be decomposed as

{L}g=\nu g-Kg

with the collision frequency
\nu(v)
and the nonlocal integral operator
K=[K_{+},K_{-}]
being defined by

\nu(v)=2Q_{loss}(1,\mu)=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}|v-u|^{\gamma}{\bf b}\left(\frac{\omega\cdot(v-u)}{|v-u|}\right)\mu(u)d\omega du\thicksim(1+|v|)^{\gamma}

(2.11)

and

\displaystyle\left(K_{\pm}g\right)(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mu}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\{2Q_{gain}\left(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{\pm},\mu\right)-Q\left(\mu,{\mu}^{\frac{1}{2}}(g_{\pm}+g_{\mp})\right)\right\}

(2.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}}|u-v|^{\gamma}{\bf b}\left(\frac{\omega\cdot(v-u)}{|v-u|}\right)\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(u)

\displaystyle\times\left\{2\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(u^{\prime})g_{\pm}(v^{\prime})-\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(v^{\prime})(g_{\pm}+g_{\mp})(u^{\prime})+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(v)(g_{\pm}+g_{\mp})(u)\right\}d\omega du,

respectively.

Under Grad’s angular cutoff assumption (
1.3
),
by
[
17
, Lemma 1]
,
L
is locally coercive in the sense that

-\left\langle f,Lf\right\rangle\geq\sigma_{0}\left|\{{\bf I}-{\bf P}\}f\right|_{\nu}^{2}\equiv\sigma_{0}\left\|\sqrt{\nu}\{{\bf I}-{\bf P}\}f\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v})},\quad\nu(v)\sim(1+|v|)^{\gamma}

(2.13)

holds for some positive constant
\sigma_{0}>0
.

To state our main result for hard sphere model, let

\left[f^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
be the unique solution of the Cauchy problem (
2.3
), (
2.4
), (
2.5
) and for some
n\in\mathbb{N},
we need to define the following energy functional
{\mathcal{E}}_{n,f^{\epsilon}}(t)
and the corresponding energy dissipation rate functional
{\mathcal{D}}_{n,f^{\epsilon}}(t)
by

{\mathcal{E}}_{n,f^{\epsilon}}(t)\sim\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f^{\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}+\left\|\left[E^{\epsilon}(t),\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t)\right]\right\|_{H^{n}_{x}}^{2}

(2.14)

and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{n,f^{\epsilon}}(t)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq|\alpha|\leq n}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\left[a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{\pm}(t),b^{f^{\epsilon}}(t),c^{f^{\epsilon}}(t)\right]\right\|^{2}+\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{\nu}+\left\|a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{+}(t)-a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{-}(t)\right\|^{2}

(2.15)

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\left\{E^{\epsilon}(t)+\epsilon b^{f^{\epsilon}}(t)\times\mathfrak{B}\right\}\right\|^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\left[\nabla_{x}E^{\epsilon}(t),\nabla_{x}\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t)\right]\right\|_{H^{n-2}_{x}}^{2},

respectively.

With the above preparations in hand, we are now ready to state our first result for the hard sphere model.

Theorem 2.1
.

For the hard sphere model, let
F^{\epsilon}(0,x,v)=\mu(v)+\sqrt{\mu(v)}f^{\epsilon}_{0}(x,v)\geq 0
, if we assume further that there exists some positive constant
c_{0}
independent of
\epsilon
such that

Y_{0}\equiv\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 3}\left\|\partial_{\beta}^{\alpha}f^{\epsilon}_{0}\right\|+\left\|E^{\epsilon}_{0}\right\|_{H^{3}_{x}}+\left\|B^{\epsilon}_{0}-\mathfrak{B}\right\|_{H^{3}_{x}}\leq c_{0},

(2.16)

then for every
\epsilon\in(0,1]
, the Cauchy problem (
2.3
), (
2.4
), (
2.5
) admits a unique global solution
\left[f^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),\right.

\left.E^{\epsilon}(t,x),\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
satisfying (
2.1
)
and the following estimates

\mathcal{E}_{3,f^{\epsilon}}(t)+\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{D}_{3,f^{\epsilon}}(\tau)d\tau\lesssim Y_{0}^{2}\lesssim c_{0}^{2}

(2.17)

holds for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

As a direct consequence of the estimate (
2.17
) and Sobolev’s imbedding theorem, one can easily deduce that the unique global solution
\left[F^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),B^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
), (
1.2
), (
1.7
), (
1.8
) obtained in Theorem
2.1
converges strongly to
\left[F^{\infty}(t,x,v),E^{\infty}(t,x),\mathcal{B}\right]
as
\epsilon\to 0_{+}
which satisfies
F^{\infty}_{\pm}(t,x,v)=\mu(v)+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(v)f^{\infty}_{\pm}(t,x,v)\geq 0
, the following estimate

\mathcal{E}^{\infty}_{3}(t)+\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{D}^{\infty}_{3}(\tau)d\tau\lesssim c_{0}^{2},

and
\left[F^{\infty}(t,x,v),E^{\infty}(t,x)\right]
solves the Cauchy problem of the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann equations (
1.1
), (
1.1
), (
1.9
) with

F^{\infty}_{0,\pm}(x,v)=\lim\limits_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}F^{\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v).

Here

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\infty}_{n}(t)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n}\left\|\partial_{\beta}^{\alpha}f^{\infty}(t)\right\|^{2}+\left\|E^{\infty}(t)\right\|^{2}_{H^{n}_{x}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\infty}_{n}(t)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq|\alpha|\leq n}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\left[a^{f^{\infty}}_{\pm}(t),b^{f^{\infty}}(t),c^{f^{\infty}}(t)\right]\right\|^{2}+\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{\infty}(t)\right\|^{2}_{\nu}+\left\|a^{f^{\infty}}_{+}(t)-a^{f^{\infty}}_{-}(t)\right\|^{2}.

Our second result in this section is to deduce the error estimate between
\left[F^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),B^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
and
\left[F^{\infty}(t,x,v),E^{\infty}(t,x),\mathcal{B}\right]
provided that certain conditions are imposed on the difference between their initial data. In fact, suppose that there exists some sufficiently small
\epsilon-
independent positive constant
\tilde{c}>0
such that

Y_{P,0}\equiv\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 4}\left\|\partial_{\beta}^{\alpha}f^{\infty}_{0}\right\|+\left\|E^{\infty}_{0}\right\|_{H^{4}_{x}}\leq\tilde{c}_{0},

(2.18)

then we can show further that
\left[f^{\infty}(t,x,v),E^{\infty}(t,x)\right]
obtained above satisfies

\mathcal{E}^{\infty}_{4}(t)+\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{D}^{\infty}_{4}(\tau)d\tau\lesssim\tilde{c}^{2},

(2.19)

from which and (
2.17
), we can get that

Corollary 2.1
.

In addition to the assumptions listed in Theorem
2.1
, we assume further that (
2.18
) holds and

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 3}\left\|\partial_{\beta}^{\alpha}\left(f^{\epsilon}_{0}-f^{\infty}_{0}\right)\right\|+\left\|E^{\epsilon}_{0}-E^{\infty}_{0}\right\|_{H^{3}_{x}}+\left\|B^{\epsilon}_{0}-\mathfrak{B}\right\|_{H^{3}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon^{\eta}

(2.20)

is true for any positive constant
\eta>0
, then we can deduce for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
that

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 3}\left\|\partial_{\beta}^{\alpha}(f^{\epsilon}(t)-f^{\infty}(t))\right\|^{2}+\left\|E^{\epsilon}(t)-E^{\infty}(t)\right\|^{2}_{H^{3}_{x}}+\left\|B^{\epsilon}(t)-\mathfrak{B}\right\|^{3}_{H^{3}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon^{\eta}.

(2.21)

2.1.2.
Main result for soft potentials

Now we turn to deal with the case of soft potentials. Recall that for the hard sphere model, we can deduce that the solution
\left[f^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
of the Cauchy problem (
2.3
), (
2.4
), (
2.5
) satisfies the estimates (
2.17
) for all
\epsilon\in(0,1]
, from which one can deduce the strong convergence of
\left[f^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),{B}^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
to
\left[f^{\infty}(t,x,v),E^{\infty}(t,x),\mathfrak{B}\right]
as
\epsilon\to 0_{+}
provided that the initial data
\left[f_{0}^{\epsilon}(x,v),E_{0}^{\epsilon}(x),\right.

\left.B^{\epsilon}_{0}-\mathfrak{B}\right]
satisfies the assumption (
2.16
).

The above argument can not be used to treat the case of soft potentials since, unlike the hard sphere model, to guarantee the global solvability of the Cauchy problem (
2.3
), (
2.4
), (
2.5
) for non-hard sphere model, a time-velocity-weighted energy method should be introduced and the temporal decay of the solution itself plays an essential role in closing the
a priori
estimates, cf.
[
5
,
7
,
12
]
and the references cited therein, and for the case when
\epsilon
is chosen sufficiently small, we had to deal with the difficulty caused by the
degeneracy of the dissipative effect of the electromagnetic field
\left[E^{\epsilon}(t,x),\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
.

To overcome such a difficulty, we need to assume that both the solution
\left[F^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),B^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
), (
1.2
), (
1.7
) and the corresponding initial data
\left[F^{\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v),E^{\epsilon}_{0}(x),B_{0}^{\epsilon}(x)\right]
satisfies the following expansions

\displaystyle F^{\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v)+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}F_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}(t,x,v)+\epsilon^{m}F^{m,\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v),

\displaystyle E^{\epsilon}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}E^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)+\epsilon^{m}E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),

(2.22)

\displaystyle B^{\epsilon}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}B^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)+\epsilon^{m}B^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),\ m\geq 1,

and

\displaystyle F^{\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F^{P,\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v)+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}F_{0,\pm}^{i,\epsilon}(x,v)+\epsilon^{m}F^{m,\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v),

\displaystyle E^{\epsilon}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{P,\epsilon}_{0}(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}E^{i,\epsilon}_{0}(x)+\epsilon^{m}E^{m,\epsilon}_{0}(x),

(2.23)

\displaystyle B^{\epsilon}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B^{P,\epsilon}_{0}(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}B^{i,\epsilon}_{0}(x)+\epsilon^{m}B^{m,\epsilon}_{0}(x),\ m\geq 1,

then we will show that
B^{P,\epsilon}(t,x),B^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)(i=1,2,\cdots,m-1),B^{P,\epsilon}_{0}(x)
, and
B^{i,\epsilon}_{0}(x)(i=1,2,\cdot,m-1)
are nothing but constant vectors (we will assume without loss of generality that all these constant vectors are independent of
\epsilon
), which will be denoted by
B^{P},B^{i}(i=1,2,\cdots,m-1)
in the rest of this paper.

In fact, by substituting the above ansatz into (
1.1
), (
1.2
) and by comparing the order of
\epsilon
in the resulting system, we can get from (
1.1
), (
1.2
), (
1.7
) that:

(i).

\left[F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v),E^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
solve the following Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system with given magnetic field
B^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{m-1}B^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}\pm E^{P,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}\pm\epsilon\left\{v\times\left(B^{P,\epsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{m-1}B^{i,\epsilon}\right)\right\}\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}

(2.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q\left(F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm},F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}\right)+Q\left(F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm},F^{P,\epsilon}_{\mp}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E^{P,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}v\left(F^{P,\epsilon}_{+}-F^{P,\epsilon}_{-}\right)dv,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}B^{P,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\times B^{P,\epsilon}=\nabla_{x}\cdot B^{P,\epsilon}=\nabla_{x}\times E^{P,\epsilon}=0,

(2.25)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{P,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\left(F^{P,\epsilon}_{+}-F^{P,\epsilon}_{-}\right)dv

with prescribed initial condition

\left[F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}(0,x,v),E^{P,\epsilon}(0,x)\right]=\left[F^{P,\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v),E^{P,\epsilon}_{0}(x)\right].

(2.26)

(
2.25
)
3
together with the Liouville theorem imply that
B^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)
is nothing but a constant vector
B^{P,\epsilon}
, while from
\eqref{vpb-condition-s}_{3}
one can conclude that there exists a potential function
\phi^{R,\epsilon}(t,x)
for the electric field
E^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)
such that
E^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)=\nabla_{x}\phi^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\lim\limits_{|x|\to+\infty}\phi^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)=0
and that the constant vector
B^{P,\epsilon}
does not depend on
\varepsilon
, thus we can set
B^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)\equiv B^{P,\epsilon}_{0}(x)\equiv B^{P}
;

(ii).

For
i=1,2,\cdots,m-1
, one can deduce that
\left[F_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{i,\epsilon}(t,x),B^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
satisfies the following linear Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann equations

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}\pm E^{i,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}\pm E^{P,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}

(2.27)

\displaystyle\pm\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=i,\atop 0<j_{1},j_{2}<i}E^{j_{1},\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{\pm}^{j_{2},\epsilon}\pm\epsilon\left\{v\times\left(B^{P}+\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{j_{1}}B^{j_{1},\epsilon}\right)\right\}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q\left(F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm},F_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}+F_{\mp}^{i,\epsilon}\right)+Q\left(F_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon},F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}+F^{P,\epsilon}_{\mp}\right)+\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=i,\atop 0<j_{1},j_{2}<i}Q\left(F_{\pm}^{j_{1},\epsilon},F_{\pm}^{j_{2},\epsilon}+F_{\mp}^{j_{2},\epsilon}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E^{i,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}}v\left(F_{+}^{i,\epsilon}-F_{-}^{i,\epsilon}\right)dv,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{i,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}}\left(F_{+}^{i,\epsilon}-F_{-}^{i,\epsilon}\right)dv,

(2.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}B^{i,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\times B^{i,\epsilon}=\nabla_{x}\cdot B^{i,\epsilon}=\nabla_{x}\times E^{i,\epsilon}=0,

and the initial data

\left[F_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}(0,x,v),E^{i,\epsilon}(0,x)\right]=\left[F_{0,\pm}^{i,\epsilon}(x,v),E_{0}^{i,\epsilon}(x)\right],

(2.29)

which satisfy the compatibility conditions

\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{0}^{i,\epsilon}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(F_{0,+}^{i,\epsilon}-F_{0,-}^{i,\epsilon}\right)dv.

(2.30)

Here again from (
2.28
)
3
together with the Liouville theorem, we can deduce for
i=1,2,\cdots,m-1
that
B^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)
are constant vectors
B^{i,\epsilon}
, while from
\eqref{F_i-s-Maxwell-con}_{3}
one can further conclude that there exist potential functions
\phi^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)
for the electric fields
E^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)
such that
E^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)=\nabla_{x}\phi^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)
hold. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\lim\limits_{|x|\to+\infty}\phi^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)=0
and that the constant vectors
B^{i,\epsilon}
does not depend on
\varepsilon
, thus we have
B^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)\equiv B^{i,\epsilon}_{0}(x)\equiv B^{i}
for
i=1,2,\cdots,m-1
;

(iii).

The remainder
\left[F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),B^{m,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
satisfies the following Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann equations

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon}\pm E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}\pm E^{P,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon}

(2.31)

\displaystyle\pm\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}\geq m,\atop 0<j_{1},j_{2}<m}\epsilon^{j_{1}+j_{2}-m}E^{j_{1},\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{\pm}^{j_{2},\epsilon}\pm\sum_{0<j_{1}<m}\epsilon^{j_{1}}E^{j_{1},\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon}\pm\sum_{0<j_{1}<m}\epsilon^{j_{1}}E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{\pm}^{j_{1},\epsilon}

\displaystyle\pm\epsilon\left(v\times B^{m,\epsilon}\right)\cdot\nabla_{v}\left\{F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}F_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}\right\}\pm\epsilon\left\{v\times\left(B^{P}+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}B^{i}\right)\right\}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon}

\displaystyle\pm\epsilon^{m}E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon}\pm\epsilon^{m+1}v\times B^{m,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q\left(F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm},F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon}+F_{\mp}^{m,\epsilon}\right)+Q\left(F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon},F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}+F_{\mp}^{P,\epsilon}\right)+\epsilon^{m}Q\left(F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon},F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon}+F_{\mp}^{m,\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}\geq m,\atop 0<j_{1},j_{2}\leq m}\epsilon^{j_{1}+j_{2}-m}Q\left(F_{\pm}^{j_{1},\epsilon},F_{\pm}^{j_{2},\epsilon}+F_{\mp}^{j_{2},\epsilon}\right),

\displaystyle\epsilon\partial_{t}E^{m,\epsilon}-\nabla_{x}\times B^{m,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\epsilon{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}}v\left(F_{+}^{m,\epsilon}-F_{-}^{m,\epsilon}\right)dv,

\displaystyle\epsilon\partial_{t}B^{m,\epsilon}+\nabla_{x}\times E^{m,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(2.32)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{m,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}}\left(F_{+}^{m,\epsilon}-F_{-}^{m,\epsilon}\right)dv,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot B^{m,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

and initial data

\displaystyle F_{\pm}^{m,\epsilon}(0,x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F_{0,\pm}^{m,\epsilon}(x,v)=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{m}}\left(F^{\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v)-F^{P,\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v)-\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}F_{0,\pm}^{i,\epsilon}(x,v)\right),

\displaystyle E^{m,\epsilon}(0,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{0}^{m,\epsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{m}}\left(E^{\epsilon}_{0}(x)-E^{P,\epsilon}_{0}(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i+1}E_{0}^{i,\epsilon}(x)\right),

(2.33)

\displaystyle B^{m,\epsilon}(0,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B_{0}^{m,\epsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{m}}\left(B^{\epsilon}_{0}(x)-B^{P}-\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}B^{i}\right),

which satisfy the compatibility conditions

\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{0}^{m,\epsilon}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(F_{0,+}^{m,\epsilon}-F_{0,-}^{m,\epsilon}\right)dv,\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot B^{m,\epsilon}_{0}=0.

(2.34)

In the perturbative framework, if we further define the perturbations
f_{\pm}^{P,\epsilon}(t,x,v)
,
f_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}(t,x,v)
for

,
f_{0,\pm}^{P,\epsilon}(x,v)
, and

for

by

\displaystyle F^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu(v)+\mu^{1/2}(v)f^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v),

\displaystyle F_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{1/2}(v)f_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}(t,x,v),\quad 1\leq i\leq m,

(2.35)

\displaystyle F^{P,\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu(v)+\mu^{1/2}(v)f^{P,\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v),

\displaystyle F_{0,\pm}^{i,\epsilon}(x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{1/2}(v)f_{0,\pm}^{i,\epsilon}(x,v),\quad 1\leq i\leq m,

and set

\displaystyle f^{P,\epsilon}(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[f^{P,\epsilon}_{+}(t,x,v),f^{P,\epsilon}_{-}(t,x,v)\right],

\displaystyle f^{i,\epsilon}(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[f^{i,\epsilon}_{+}(t,x,v),f^{i,\epsilon}_{-}(t,x,v)\right],\quad 1\leq i\leq m,

\displaystyle f^{P,\epsilon}_{0}(x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[f^{P,\epsilon}_{0,+}(x,v),f^{P,\epsilon}_{0,-}(x,v)\right],

\displaystyle f^{i,\epsilon}_{0}(x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[f^{i,\epsilon}_{0,+}(x,v),f^{i,\epsilon}_{0,-}(x,v)\right],\quad 1\leq i\leq m,

then, noticing that

B^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)=B^{P},\quad B^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)=B^{i},\quad i=1,2,\cdots,m-1,

we can get from (
2.24
)-(

), (

)-(

), and

)-(

that

(i).

\left[f^{P,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
satisfies the following Cauchy problem

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f^{P,\epsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f^{P,\epsilon}-E^{P,\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}q_{1}

(2.36)

\displaystyle+q_{0}E^{P,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{P,\epsilon}+q_{0}\epsilon\left\{v\times\left(B^{P}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{j}B^{j}\right)\right\}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{P,\epsilon}+{L}f^{P,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}q_{0}E^{P,\epsilon}\cdot vf^{P,\epsilon}+{\Gamma}\left(f^{P,\epsilon},f^{P,\epsilon}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E^{P,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}v\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(v)\left(f^{P,\epsilon}_{+}-f^{P,\epsilon}_{-}\right)dv,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{P,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(v)\left(f^{P,\epsilon}_{+}-f^{P,\epsilon}_{-}\right)dv,\quad\nabla_{x}\times E^{P,\epsilon}=0,

\left[f^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}(0,x,v),E^{P,\epsilon}(0,x)\right]=\left[f^{P,\epsilon}_{0,\pm}(x,v),E^{P,\epsilon}_{0}(x)\right];

(2.37)

(ii).

For
i=1,2,\cdots,m-1
,
\left[f^{i,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{i,\epsilon}(t,x),B^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
with
B^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)=B^{i}
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f^{i,\epsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f^{i,\epsilon}-E^{i,\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}q_{1}-\frac{1}{2}q_{0}E^{i,\epsilon}\cdot vf^{P,\epsilon}+q_{0}E^{i,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{P,\epsilon}

(2.38)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}q_{0}E^{P,\epsilon}\cdot vf^{i,\epsilon}+q_{0}E^{P,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{i,\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=i,\atop 0<j_{1},j_{2}<i}q_{0}E^{j_{1},\epsilon}\cdot vf^{j_{2},\epsilon}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=i,\atop 0<j_{1},j_{2}<i}q_{0}E^{j_{1},\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{j_{2},\epsilon}+\epsilon q_{0}\left\{v\times\left(B^{P}+\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{j}B^{j}\right)\right\}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{i,\epsilon}+{L}f^{i,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\Gamma}\left(f^{P,\epsilon},f^{i,\epsilon}\right)+{\Gamma}\left(f^{i,\epsilon},f^{P,\epsilon}\right)+\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=i,\atop 0<j_{1},j_{2}<i}\Gamma\left(f^{j_{1},\epsilon},f^{j_{2},\epsilon}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E^{i,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}v\mu^{1/2}(v)\left(f_{+}^{i,\epsilon}-f_{-}^{i,\epsilon}\right)dv,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\times E^{i,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(2.39)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{i,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\mu^{1/2}(v)\left(f_{+}^{i,\epsilon}-f_{-}^{i,\epsilon}\right)dv,

and the initial condition

\left[f^{i,\epsilon}(0,x,v),E^{i,\epsilon}(0,x)\right]=\left[f_{0}^{i,\epsilon}(x,v),E_{0}^{i,\epsilon}(x)\right],

(2.40)

which satisfy the compatibility conditions

\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{0}^{i,\epsilon}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mu^{1/2}(v)\left(f_{0,+}^{i,\epsilon}-f_{0,-}^{i,\epsilon}\right)dv;

(2.41)

(iii).

\left[f^{m,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),B^{m,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f^{m,\epsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f^{m,\epsilon}-E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}q_{1}-\frac{1}{2}q_{0}E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot vf^{P,\epsilon}+q_{0}E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{P,\epsilon}

(2.42)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}q_{0}E^{P,\epsilon}\cdot vf^{m,\epsilon}+q_{0}E^{P,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{m,\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2}q_{0}\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}\geq m,\atop 0<j_{1},j_{2}<m}\epsilon^{j_{1}+j_{2}-m}E^{j_{1},\epsilon}\cdot vf^{j_{2},\epsilon}

\displaystyle+q_{0}\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}\geq m,\atop 0<j_{1},j_{2}<m}\epsilon^{j_{1}+j_{2}-m}E^{j_{1},\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{j_{2},\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2}q_{0}\sum_{0<j_{1}<m}\epsilon^{j_{1}}E^{j_{1},\epsilon}\cdot vf^{m,\epsilon}

\displaystyle+q_{0}\sum_{0<j_{1}<m}\epsilon^{j_{1}}E^{j_{1},\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{m,\epsilon}-q_{0}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{0<j_{1}<m}\epsilon^{j_{1}}E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot vf^{j_{1},\epsilon}+q_{0}\sum_{0<j_{1}<m}\epsilon^{j_{1}}E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{j_{1},\epsilon}

\displaystyle+\epsilon\left(v\times B^{m,\epsilon}\right)\cdot\nabla_{v}\left\{f^{P,\epsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}f^{i,\epsilon}\right\}+q_{0}\epsilon\left\{v\times\left(B^{P}+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}B^{i}\right)\right\}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{m,\epsilon}

\displaystyle-\frac{\epsilon^{m}}{2}q_{0}E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot vf^{m,\epsilon}+q_{0}\epsilon^{m}E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{m,\epsilon}+q_{0}\epsilon^{m+1}v\times B^{m,\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}f^{m,\epsilon}+{L}f^{m,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\Gamma}\left(f^{P,\epsilon},f^{m,\epsilon}\right)+{\Gamma}\left(f^{m,\epsilon},f^{P,\epsilon}\right)+\epsilon^{m}{\Gamma}\left(f^{m,\epsilon},f^{m,\epsilon}\right)+\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}\geq m,\atop 0<j_{1},j_{2}\leq m}\epsilon^{j_{1}+j_{2}-m}\Gamma\left(f^{j_{1},\epsilon},f^{j_{2},\epsilon}\right),

\displaystyle\epsilon\partial_{t}E^{m,\epsilon}-\nabla_{x}\times B^{m,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}v\mu^{1/2}(v)\left(f_{+}^{m,\epsilon}-f_{-}^{m,\epsilon}\right)dv,

\displaystyle\epsilon\partial_{t}B^{m,\epsilon}+\nabla_{x}\times E^{m,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(2.43)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\cdot E^{m,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\mu^{1/2}(v)\left(f_{+}^{m,\epsilon}-f_{-}^{m,\epsilon}\right)dv,\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot B^{m,\epsilon}=0

with prescribed initial data

\left[f^{m,\epsilon}(0,x,v),E^{m,\epsilon}(0,x),B^{m,\epsilon}(0,x)\right]=\left[f_{0}^{m,\epsilon}(x,v),E_{0}^{m,\epsilon}(x),B_{0}^{m,\epsilon}(x)\right],

(2.44)

which satisfy the compatibility conditions

\nabla_{x}\cdot E_{0}^{m,\epsilon}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mu^{1/2}(v)\left(f_{0,+}^{m,\epsilon}-f_{0,-}^{m,\epsilon}\right)dv,\quad\nabla_{x}\cdot B_{0}^{m,\epsilon}=0.

(2.45)

Now let
\left[f^{m,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),{B}^{m,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
be the unique solution of the Cauchy problem (
2.42
)-(
2.45
) and for
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
k\leq n
, we can define the following energy functionals
\mathcal{E}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n}(t),

\mathcal{E}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),

\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),

\mathcal{E}^{k}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n}(t),

\mathcal{E}^{k}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),

\mathcal{E}^{(n,j)}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)
,
\mathcal{E}^{(n,0)}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)
,
\mathcal{E}^{(0,0)}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)
, and the corresponding energy dissipation rate functionals
\mathcal{D}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n}(t)
,
\mathcal{D}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)
,
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)
,
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n}(t)
,
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)
,
\mathcal{D}^{(n,j)}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)
,
\mathcal{D}^{(n,0)}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)
,
\mathcal{D}^{(0,0)}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|\leq n}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\left[f^{m,\epsilon}(t),E^{m,\epsilon}(t),B^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right]\right\|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,-\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n}(t),

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)+\left\|\Lambda^{-\varrho}\left[f^{m,\epsilon}(t),E^{m,\epsilon}(t),B^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right]\right\|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{k}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{k\leq|\alpha|\leq n}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\left[f^{m,\epsilon}(t),E^{m,\epsilon}(t),B^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right]\right\|^{2},

(2.46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{k}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n,\atop|\alpha|\geq k}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,-\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}+\mathcal{E}^{k}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n}(t),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(n,j)}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=n}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,1}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2},\quad 1\leq|\beta|=j\leq n,n\geq 1,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(n,0)}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=n}\left\|w_{\ell,1}\partial^{\alpha}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2},\quad n\geq 1,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(0,0)}_{f^{m,\epsilon},0,\ell,1}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\left\|w_{\ell,1}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2},

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|\leq n}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|_{\nu}^{2}+\left\|\nabla_{x}{\bf P}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{H^{n-1}_{x}}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\nabla_{x}\left[E^{m,\epsilon}(t),B^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right]\right\|_{H^{n-2}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\left\|E^{m,\epsilon}(t)+\epsilon b^{f^{m,\epsilon}}(t)\times\left\{B^{P}+\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{j}B^{j}\right\}\right\|^{2}+\left\|a^{f^{m,\epsilon}}_{+}(t)-a^{f^{m,\epsilon}}_{-}(t)\right\|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,-\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{\nu}+\mathcal{D}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n}(t)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(1+t)^{1+\vartheta}}\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,-\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\langle v\rangle\right\|^{2},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)+\left\|\Lambda^{-\varrho}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|_{\nu}^{2}

(2.47)

\displaystyle\left\|\Lambda^{-\varrho}\left[a^{f^{m,\epsilon}}_{+}(t)-a^{f^{m,\epsilon}}_{-}(t)\right]\right\|^{2}+\left\|\Lambda^{1-\varrho}\left[{\bf P}f^{m,\epsilon}(t),\epsilon E^{m,\epsilon}(t),\epsilon B^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right]\right\|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{k}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{k\leq|\alpha|\leq n}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}{\{\bf I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|_{\nu}^{2}+\left\|\nabla^{k+1}_{x}{\bf P}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{H^{n-1-k}_{x}}

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\nabla^{k+1}_{x}\left[E^{m,\epsilon}(t),B^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right]\right\|_{H^{n-2-k}_{x}}^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{k}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n,|\alpha|\geq k}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,-\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{\nu}+\mathcal{D}^{k}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n}(t)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(1+t)^{1+\vartheta}}\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n,|\alpha|\geq k}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,-\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\langle v\rangle\right\|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(n,j)}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=n}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,1}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|_{\nu}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(1+t)^{1+\vartheta}}\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=n}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,1}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\langle v\rangle\right\|^{2},1\leq|\beta|=j\leq n,n\geq 1,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(n,0)}_{f^{m,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=n}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,1}\partial^{\alpha}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|_{\nu}^{2}+\frac{1}{(1+t)^{1+\vartheta}}\sum_{|\alpha|=n}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,1}\partial^{\alpha}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\langle v\rangle\right\|^{2},\ n\geq 1,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(0,0)}_{f^{m,\epsilon},0,\ell,1}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,1}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|_{\nu}^{2}+\frac{1}{(1+t)^{1+\vartheta}}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,1}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\langle v\rangle\right\|^{2}.

Similarly, let
\left[f^{P,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
be the unique solution of the Cauchy problems (
2.36
), (
2.37
) and for
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
k\leq n
, we can also define the following energy functionals
\mathcal{E}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n}(t),\mathcal{E}^{k}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n}(t),\mathcal{E}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),

\mathcal{E}^{k}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)
,
\mathcal{E}^{(n,j)}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)
and the corresponding energy dissipation rate functionals
\mathcal{D}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n}(t),

\mathcal{D}^{k}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n}(t),

\mathcal{D}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),\mathcal{D}^{k}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)
,
\mathcal{D}^{(n,j)}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{k}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k\leq|\alpha|\leq n}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\left[f^{P,\epsilon}(t),E^{P,\epsilon}(t)\right]\right\|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{0}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n}(t),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{k}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n,|\alpha|\geq k}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,-\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f^{P,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}+\mathcal{E}^{k}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n}(t),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{0}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)+\left\|\Lambda^{-\varrho}\left[f^{P,\epsilon}(t),E^{P,\epsilon}(t)\right]\right\|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(n,j)}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=n}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,1}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{P,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2},\quad 1\leq|\beta|=j\leq n,n\geq 1,

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{k}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k\leq|\alpha|\leq n}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\{{\bf I}-{\bf P}\}f^{P,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{\nu}+\left\|\nabla_{x}{\bf P}f^{P,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{H^{n-1}_{x}}+\left\|a_{+}^{f^{P,\epsilon}}(t)-a_{-}^{f^{P,\epsilon}}(t)\right\|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{0}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n}(t),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{k}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n,|\alpha|\geq k}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,-\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\{{\bf I}-{\bf P}\}f^{P,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{\nu}+\mathcal{D}^{k}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n}(t)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(1+t)^{1+\vartheta}}\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq n,|\alpha|\geq k}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,-\gamma}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\{{\bf I}-{\bf P}\}f^{P,\epsilon}(t)\langle v\rangle\right\|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{0}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)+\left\|\Lambda^{-\varrho}\left[f^{P,\epsilon}(t),E^{P,\epsilon}(t)\right]\right\|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(n,j)}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=n}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,1}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{P,\epsilon}(t)\right\|_{\nu}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(1+t)^{1+\vartheta}}\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=n}\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,1}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\bf\{I-P\}}f^{P,\epsilon}(t)\langle v\rangle\right\|^{2},1\leq|\beta|=j\leq n,n\geq 1.

Moreover, for the solution
\left[f^{i,\epsilon}(t,x,v),\right.

\left.E^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]

(i=1,2,\cdots,m-1)
of the Cauchy problem
(
2.38
)-(
2.41
), the energy functionals
\mathcal{E}_{f^{i,\epsilon},n}(t),\mathcal{E}^{k}_{f^{i,\epsilon},n}(t),\mathcal{E}_{f^{i,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),

\mathcal{E}^{k}_{f^{i,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{f^{P,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)
,
\mathcal{E}^{(n,j)}_{f^{i,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)
and the corresponding energy dissipation rate functionals
\mathcal{D}_{f^{i,\epsilon},n}(t),\mathcal{D}^{k}_{f^{i,\epsilon},n}(t),

\mathcal{D}_{f^{i,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),\mathcal{D}^{k}_{f^{i,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t),\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{f^{i,\epsilon},n,\ell,-\gamma}(t)
,
\mathcal{D}^{(n,j)}_{f^{i,\epsilon},n,\ell,1}(t)
can be defined in the same way.

With the above preparations in hand, we are now ready to state our main result for cutoff soft potentials.

Theorem 2.2
.

Assume that

•

\gamma\in(-3,-1)
,
\varrho\in\left[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)
and

m>\frac{5}{2}+\varrho
;

•

For any given constants vectors
B^{P},B^{i}(i=1,2,\cdots,m-1)
independent of
\epsilon
, it holds that

\displaystyle F^{\epsilon}_{0}(x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu+\sqrt{\mu}\left(f^{P,\epsilon}_{0}(x,v)+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}f_{0}^{i,\epsilon}(x,v)+\epsilon^{m}f_{0}^{m,\epsilon}(x,v)\right)\geq 0,

\displaystyle E^{\epsilon}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{P,\epsilon}_{0}(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}E_{0}^{i,\epsilon}(x)+\epsilon^{m}E_{0}^{m,\epsilon}(x),

(2.48)

\displaystyle B^{\epsilon}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B^{P}+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}B^{i}+\epsilon^{m}B_{0}^{m,\epsilon}(x);

•

The parameters
l_{m}^{\sharp},l_{m}^{*},l_{i}^{*}(i=1,2,\cdots,m-1),l_{P}^{*}
are suitably chosen sufficiently large positive constants whose precise ranges will be specified in the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

Then if we assume further that there exists a sufficiently small positive constant
c_{1}
, which is independent of
\epsilon
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{Y}_{\textrm{Total},0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq N^{0}_{m}}\left\|\langle v\rangle^{l_{m}^{\sharp}-|\beta|}{e^{q\langle v\rangle^{2}}}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f_{0}^{m,\epsilon}\right\|+\sum_{N_{0}+1\leq|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq N_{m}+1}\left\|\langle v\rangle^{l_{m}^{*}-|\beta|}{e^{q\langle v\rangle^{2}}}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f_{0}^{m,\epsilon}\right\|

(2.49)

\displaystyle+\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq N_{i}+1,\atop 1\leq i\leq m-1}\left\|\langle v\rangle^{l_{i}^{*}-|\beta|}{e^{q\langle v\rangle^{2}}}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f_{0}^{i,\epsilon}\right\|+\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq N_{P}}\left\|\langle v\rangle^{l^{*}_{P}-|\beta|}e^{q\langle v\rangle^{2}}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}f^{P,\epsilon}_{0}\right\|

\displaystyle+\sum_{1\leq i\leq m-1}\left\|\left[f_{0}^{m,\epsilon},f_{0}^{i,\epsilon},f^{P,\epsilon}_{0},E_{0}^{m,\epsilon},E_{0}^{i,\epsilon},E^{P,\epsilon}_{0},B_{0}^{m,\epsilon}\right]\right\|_{\dot{H}^{-\varrho}}\leq c_{1},

we can deduce that

•

For
i=1,2,\cdots,m-1
, the Cauchy problems (
2.36
)-(
2.37
), (
2.38
)-(
2.41
), and (
2.42
)-(
2.45
) admit unique global solutions
\left[f^{P,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
,
\left[f^{i,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
and
\left[f^{m,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),\right.

\left.B^{m,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
, respectively;

•

The Cauchy problem (
1.1
), (
1.2
), (
1.7
), (
1.8
) admits a unique global solution
\left[F^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),B^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
which satisfies

\displaystyle F^{\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu(v)+\sqrt{\mu(v)}\left(f^{P,\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v)+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}f_{\pm}^{i,\epsilon}(t,x,v)+\epsilon^{m}f^{m,\epsilon}_{\pm}(t,x,v)\right)\geq 0,

\displaystyle E^{\epsilon}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}E^{i,\epsilon}(t,x)+\epsilon^{m}E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),

\displaystyle B^{\epsilon}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B^{P}+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{i}B^{i}+\epsilon^{m}B^{m,\epsilon}(t,x).

Furthermore, we have

\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq N_{m}}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\left\{\frac{F^{\epsilon}-F^{P,\epsilon}}{\mu^{1/2}}\right\}\right\|^{2}+\left\|E^{\epsilon}-E^{P,\epsilon}\right\|_{H^{N_{m}+1}_{x}}^{2}+\left\|B^{\epsilon}-B^{P}\right\|^{2}_{H^{N_{m}+1}_{x}}\lesssim\epsilon^{2}\mathbb{Y}_{\textrm{Total},0}^{2}.

(2.50)

Remark 2.1
.

Some remarks concerning Theorem
2.2
are listed below:

(i).

Although only the case of
-3<\gamma<-1
is studied in Theorem
2.2
, the case of
-1\leq\gamma<1
is much more simpler and similar result holds also. In fact in such a case, one can close the desired energy type estimates by employing only one set of weighted energy method with the weight
w_{\ell-|\beta|,|\gamma|}(t,v)=\langle v\rangle^{|\gamma|(\ell-|\beta|)}e^{\frac{q\langle v\rangle^{2}}{(1+t)^{\vartheta}}}
;

(ii).

Under the assumptions imposed in Theorem
2.2
, since we do not know whether
F^{P,\epsilon}_{0}(x,v)=\mu(v)+\sqrt{\mu(v)}f_{0}^{P,\epsilon}(x,v)
is nonnegative or not, thus we can not deduce that
F^{P,\epsilon}(t,x,v)=\mu(v)+\sqrt{\mu(v)}f^{P,\epsilon}(t,x,v)
is nonnegative, but we can really prove that, as the parameter
\epsilon\to 0_{+}
,
\left[F^{P,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{P,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
will converge strongly to the unique global-in-time solution
\left[F^{\infty}(t,x,v),E^{\infty}(t,x)\right]
of the Cauchy problem of the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system (
1.1
)-(
1.1
) with prescribed initial data (
1.9
). Here
F^{\infty}_{0}(x,v)=\lim\limits_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}F^{\epsilon}_{0}(x,v)
. Since
F^{\epsilon}_{0}(x,v)\geq 0
, we can thus get that
F^{\infty}_{0}(x,v)\geq 0
and from which one can further prove that
F^{\infty}(t,x,v)\geq 0
;

(iii).

The arguments used in this paper can also be used to study the global-in-time Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann limit of the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) for the whole range of non-cutoff intermolecular interactions;

(iv).

The parameters
l_{m}^{\sharp},l_{m}^{*},l_{i}^{*}(i=1,2,\cdots,m-1),l_{P}^{*}
in Theorem
2.2
satisfy the following relations:

–

\vartheta
satisfies

0<\vartheta\leq\min\left\{\frac{\gamma-2}{4\gamma-2}\left(\frac{5}{2}+\varrho\right)-1,\frac{\varrho}{2}-\frac{1}{4}\right\};

–

Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
be a sufficiently small fixed constant and the constants
N_{P},N_{i},N_{m}
, and
\sigma_{n,k}
are chosen to satisfy

*

N_{m}^{0}\geq 5
and
N_{m}+1=2N_{m}^{0}-1+[\varrho]
;

*

N_{i}\geq N_{i+1}+2
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
,
N_{P}\geq N_{1}+2
;

*

\sigma_{n,0}=0
for
n\leq N_{m}
and
\sigma_{N_{m}+1,0}=\frac{1+\epsilon_{0}}{2}
, and

\sigma_{n,k}-\sigma_{n,k-1}=\frac{2(1+\gamma)}{\gamma-2}(1+\vartheta),1\leq k\leq n,1\leq n\leq N_{m}+1;

–

There exist properly large constants
\widehat{l}_{i},\widehat{l}_{m}
and the parameters
l_{P},l_{i},l_{m}
satisifies

*

\widehat{l}_{m}>\frac{N_{P}+\varrho}{2}
,

\widehat{l}_{m-1}\geq\widehat{l}_{m},\widehat{l}_{j-1}\geq\widehat{l}_{j}
for
2\leq j\leq m-1
,
\widehat{l}^{P}\geq\widehat{l}_{1}
;

*

\widetilde{\ell}_{m}\geq\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{(2-4\gamma)\sigma_{N_{m},N_{m}}}{1+\varrho}
,
l_{m}\geq N_{m}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\gamma}
;

*

l_{m}^{*}\geq\widetilde{\ell}_{m}-\gamma l_{m}+1+\frac{\gamma}{2}
,

\ell_{m}\geq-\frac{l^{*}_{m}+1}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}
,
l_{m}^{0}\geq\ell_{m}+\widehat{l}_{m}
,
l_{m}^{\sharp}\geq\widetilde{\ell}_{m}-\gamma l^{0}_{m}+1+\frac{\gamma}{2}
;

*

\widetilde{\ell}_{m-1}\geq\frac{\gamma}{2}-\frac{2\gamma\sigma_{N_{m-1},N_{m-1}}}{1+\varrho}
,
\ell_{m-1}\geq N_{m}^{0}+3-\frac{l_{m}^{\sharp}+1-N_{m}^{0}}{\gamma}
,
l_{m-1}\geq\ell_{m-1}+\widehat{\ell}_{m-1}
and
l_{m-1}^{*}\geq\widetilde{\ell}_{m-1}-\gamma l_{m-1}
;

*

\widetilde{\ell}_{j}\geq\frac{\gamma}{2}-\frac{2\gamma\sigma_{N_{j},N_{j}}}{1+\varrho}
,
\ell_{j}\geq-\frac{l_{j+1}^{*}}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\gamma}
,
l_{j}\geq\ell_{j}+\widehat{l}_{j}
and
l_{j}^{*}\geq\widetilde{\ell}_{j}-\gamma l_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq m-2
;

*

\widetilde{\ell}^{P}\geq\frac{\gamma}{2}-\frac{2\gamma\sigma_{N^{P},N^{P}}}{1+\varrho}
,
\ell_{P}\geq-\frac{l_{1}^{*}}{\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{\gamma}
,
l_{P}\geq\ell_{P}+\widehat{l}_{P}
and
l_{P}^{*}\geq\widetilde{\ell}_{P}-\gamma l_{P}
.

2.2.
Main ideas

Now we outline our main ideas to deduce our main results, Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
2.2
. Our method is based on the nonlinear energy method developed recently in
[
18
,
28
,
29
]
to deal with the global solvability of the Boltzmann equation in the perturbative framework. Such a method is based on the so-called macro-microscopic decompositions of the solution of the Boltzmann equation and the equation itself and it has been proved to be effective to be used to yield the global solvability of some complex kinetic equations, cf.
[
5
,
7
,
9
,
12
,
17
,
20
,
24
,
27
,
32
,
33
]
for the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system (
1.1
), (
1.2
) and

[
6
,
8
,
10
,
11
,
15
,
26
,
35
,
36
,
37
,
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
42
]
for the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system (
1.1
), (
1.1
), etc.

Since the main purpose of this paper is to give a rigorous mathematical justification of the global-in-time limit from the Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system (
1.1
), (
1.2
) to the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system (
1.1
),
we need to deduce certain
a priori
estimates on the solution
\left[f^{\epsilon}_{+}(t,x,v),f^{\epsilon}_{-}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
of the Cauchy problem of (
2.3
), (
2.4
), (
2.5
) which are independent of the parameter
\epsilon=\frac{1}{c}
.

For Theorem
2.1
, the main difficulty is due to degeneracy of the dissipative effect of the electromagnetic field
\left[E^{\epsilon}(t,x),\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
for small
\epsilon=\frac{1}{c}
and such a fact is reflected in the expression of the energy dissipation rate functional
{\mathcal{D}}_{n,f^{\epsilon}}(t)
defined by (
2.15
), i.e. the term

\epsilon^{2}\left\|\left\{E^{\epsilon}(t)+\epsilon b^{f^{\epsilon}}(t)\times\mathfrak{B}\right\}\right\|^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\left\|\left[\nabla_{x}E^{\epsilon}(t),\nabla_{x}\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t)\right]\right\|_{H^{m-2}_{x}}^{2}

in the right hand side of (
2.15
).

Such a degeneracy effect does lead to some difficulties on the energy types estimate related to the term
E^{\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}q_{1}
in (
2.6
) induced by
E^{\epsilon}\cdot\nabla_{v}F^{\epsilon}
. In fact, if one performs the energy estimates directly to (
2.6
), such a term does not cause any inconvenience since

-\left(\partial^{\alpha}\left(E^{\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}q_{1}\right),\partial^{\alpha}f^{\epsilon}\right)=\frac{d}{dt}\left\{\left\|\partial^{\alpha}E^{\epsilon}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\partial^{\alpha}\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}\right\|^{2}\right\},

while for the corresponding estimates on the terms in which mix-derivative
\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}
on
\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{\epsilon}
is involved, one has to perform the microscopic projection
\{{\bf I-P}\}
to (
2.6
) and noticing that
E^{\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}q_{1}
remains unchanged under such a microscopic projection, thus one has to treat the linear term

\left(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\left(E^{\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}q_{1}\right),\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{\epsilon}\right)

the argument used in
[
5
,
7
,
9
,
17
,
32
]
can not be applied any more to yield the desired
\epsilon-
independent estimates. To overcome such a difficulty, we note that

\{{\bf I-P}\}\left\{E^{\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}q_{1}-q_{0}\epsilon(v\times\mathfrak{B})\cdot\nabla_{v}{\bf P}f^{\epsilon}\right\}=\left\{E^{\epsilon}+\epsilon b^{f^{\epsilon}}\times\mathfrak{B}\right\}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}q_{1},

one has from Lemma
3.6
that

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{\beta}^{\alpha}\left\{q_{1}\left\{E^{\epsilon}+\epsilon b^{f^{\epsilon}}\times\mathfrak{B}\right\}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}\right\},\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\{\bf I-P\}}f^{\epsilon}\right)

(2.51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\beta|}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{3}\left(\partial^{\alpha}\left\{E^{\epsilon}+\epsilon b^{f^{\epsilon}}\times\mathfrak{B}\right\}_{i},\left\langle\partial_{2\beta}\left\{v_{i}\mu^{1/2}\right\},\partial^{\alpha}{\{\bf I_{+}-P_{+}\}}f^{\epsilon}-\partial^{\alpha}{\{\bf I_{-}-P_{-}\}}f^{\epsilon}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4C_{\beta}}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\left\langle\partial_{2\beta}\left\{v_{i}\mu^{1/2}\right\},\partial^{\alpha}{\{\bf I_{+}-P_{+}\}}f^{\epsilon}(t)-\partial^{\alpha}{\{\bf I_{-}-P_{-}\}}f^{\epsilon}(t)\right\rangle\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|\nabla^{|\alpha|}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla^{|\alpha|+1}f^{\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{3,f^{\epsilon}}(t)\mathcal{D}_{3,f^{\epsilon}}(t).

and then such a term can be controlled suitably. Here
C_{\beta}
are some positive constants and
\mathcal{E}_{3,f^{\epsilon}}(t),\mathcal{D}_{3,f^{\epsilon}}(t)
are defined by (
2.14
) and (
2.15
), respectively.

As for the proof of Theorem
2.2
, since our main purpose is to cover the whole range of intermolecular interactions, the dissipative effect induced by the coercivity estimate (
2.13
) of the linearized Boltzmann collision operator
L
for non-hard sphere model is weaker than the hard sphere model and such a dissipative effect is not sufficient to control the corresponding nonlinear terms induced by the Lorentz forces, which can lead to the velocity growth at the rate of
|v|
. To overcome such a difficulty, a general strategy developed in
[
5
,
7
]
is to introduce some weights of the form
w_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}(t,v)=\langle v\rangle^{\kappa(\ell-|\beta|)}e^{\frac{q\langle v\rangle^{2}}{(1+t)^{\vartheta}}}
to compensate the degeneracy coercivity property of the linearized Boltzmann collision operator
Lf
.

As a consequence of the introduction of the weight
w_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}(t,v)
, we can now deduce the following two types of dissipative mechanisms for the non-fluid component
\{{\bf I}-{\bf P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}
:

•

The first one is the dissipative term

D^{L}_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}(f^{m,\epsilon})\equiv\left\|w_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\{{\bf I}-{\bf P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}\right\|^{2}_{\nu}

(2.52)

which is due to the coercive estimate of
L
;

•

The second type is the extra dissipative term

D^{W}_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}(f^{m,\epsilon})\equiv(1+t)^{-1-\vartheta}\left\|\langle v\rangle w_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\{{\bf I}-{\bf P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}\right\|^{2}

(2.53)

induced by the exponent factor of the weight
w_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}(t,v)
.

With the above two types of dissipative effects on the non-fluid component
\{{\bf I}-{\bf P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}
, the nonlinear terms related to the Lorentz force can be estimates suitably as in
[
5
,
7
]

if one can show that the electromagnetic fields
\left[E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),B^{m,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
enjoy certain temporal decay estimates. We note, however, that, since the influence of the light speed, if we employ the interpolation technique introduced in
[
19
]
to yield the desired temporal decay estimates, we can only deduce that the temporal decay estimates of the electromagnetic fields
\left[E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),B^{m,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
do depend on light speed. In fact one can only show that the electric field
E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x)
enjoy the following temporal decay rates

\left\|\nabla^{k}E^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}\lesssim\epsilon^{-2(k+\varrho)}(1+t)^{-(k+\varrho)}

(2.54)

and such a decay estimate can not guarantee that the corresponding term related to
E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot vf^{m,\epsilon}
can be bounded suitably which is due to the appearance of the factor
\epsilon^{-2(k+\varrho)}
in (
2.54
).

Our idea to go on is to use the ansatz (
2.1.2
) to expand
\left[F^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),B^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
to certain order. Our observation is that if
m
, the order of expansion, is taken suitably large, such an expansion (
2.1.2
) will leads to the appearance of the factor
\epsilon^{m}
in front of
E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot vf^{m,\epsilon}
and such a term now can be estimated as follows:

\displaystyle\left(\epsilon^{m}E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot v\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{m,\epsilon},w^{2}_{\ell,\kappa}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}\right)

(2.55)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\epsilon^{m}\left\|E^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\left\|w_{\ell,\kappa}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\langle v\rangle\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\epsilon^{m}\epsilon^{-\varrho-\frac{3}{2}}(1+t)^{-\frac{3}{4}-\frac{\varrho}{2}}\left\|w_{\ell,1}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\langle v\rangle\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\epsilon^{m-\varrho-\frac{3}{2}}(1+t)^{-1-\vartheta}\left\|w_{\ell,\kappa}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\langle v\rangle\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(1+t)^{-1-\vartheta}\left\|w_{\ell,\kappa}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\langle v\rangle\right\|^{2}

if
m\geq\rho+\frac{3}{2}
. Consequently, the above term can be absorbed by the extra dissipative term
D^{W}_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}(f^{m,\epsilon})
defined by (
2.53
), which is induced by the exponential part of the s
w_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}(t,v)
.

Although the above analysis has shown the power of the above-mentioned weighted energy method based on the weight functions
w_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}(t,v)
in studying the global solvability of the Cauchy problem
(
2.42
)-(
2.45
), we note, however, that, as pointed out in
[
5
,
7
]
, we can not hope to close the desired energy estimates by using a single weight
w_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}(t,v)
with some specifically chosen constant
\kappa
. More precisely, we can not hope to control the terms related both to the nonlinear term induced by the magnetic field
B^{m,\epsilon}(t,x)

\left(\left(v\times\partial^{\alpha-e_{j}}B^{m,\epsilon}\right)\cdot\nabla_{v}\partial^{\alpha+e_{j}}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{m,\epsilon},w_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}\right)

(2.56)

and to the linear transport term
v\cdot\nabla_{x}f^{m,\epsilon}(t,x,v)

\left(\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\left\{v\cdot\nabla_{x}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}\right\},w_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}\right)

(2.57)

simultaneously by using the dissipative effects (
2.52
) and (
2.53
) for all
-3<\gamma\leq 1
and for just one suitably chosen
constant
\kappa
.

In fact, for
-3<\gamma<0
, if we want to control (
2.57
) suitably first by (
2.52
), one has to choose
\kappa=-\gamma
, but if we try to use (
2.53
) to bound (
2.56
) with
\kappa=-\gamma
, we had to assume that
\gamma\geq-1
.

Our main idea, as in
[
5
,
7
]
, is to introduce the following two sets of weighted energy estimates:

(i).

The weighted energy method with respect to the weight
w_{\ell-|\beta|,-\gamma}(t,v)
is used to deduce the necessary temporal decay estimates on
\left[f^{m,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),B^{m,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
together with the uniform-in-time weighted energy type estimates;

(ii).

The weighted energy method with respect to the weight
w_{\ell-|\beta|,1}(t,v)
is used to yield the time increase rates on the weighted energy type estimates on
\left[f^{m,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),B^{m,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
.

By employing these two sets of weighted energy methods and by suitably interpolations between these two sets of weighted norms, we can indeed obtain the desired energy type estimates and show that the solution
\left[f^{m,\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),B^{m,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
satisfies the desired temporal decay estimates like (
2.54
).

At last, similar to that of hard sphere model, we had to deal with the estimates related to the term
E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}q_{1}
, which is due to the degeneracy of the dissipative effect of the electromagnetic field
\left[E^{m,\epsilon}(t,x),B^{m,\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
for small
\epsilon=\frac{1}{c}
.
Compared with the hard sphere model, the difference now is that we had to treat the following weighted estimate:

\left(\partial_{\beta}^{\alpha}\left\{q_{1}E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}\right\},w^{2}_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\{\bf I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}\right).

To control such a term, we note that

\{{\bf I-P}\}\left\{E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}q_{1}-q_{0}\epsilon(v\times\mathfrak{B})\cdot\nabla_{v}{\bf P}f^{m,\epsilon}\right\}=\left\{E^{m,\epsilon}+\epsilon b^{f^{m,\epsilon}}\times\mathfrak{B}\right\}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}q_{1}

(2.58)

and

E^{m,\epsilon}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}q_{1}-q_{0}\epsilon(v\times\mathfrak{B})\cdot\nabla_{v}{\bf P}f^{m,\epsilon}=\left\{E^{m,\epsilon}+\epsilon b^{f^{m,\epsilon}}\times\mathfrak{B}\right\}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}q_{1},

(2.59)

we can therefore get from Lemma
4.3
that

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{\beta}^{\alpha}\left\{q_{1}\left\{E^{m,\epsilon}+\epsilon b^{f^{m,\epsilon}}\times\left\{B^{P}+\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{k}B^{k}\right\}\right\}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}\right\},w^{2}_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}\partial^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\{\bf I-P\}}f^{m,\epsilon}\right)

(2.60)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4C_{\beta}}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{d}{dt}\left\|\left\langle\partial_{\beta}\left\{w^{2}_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}\partial_{\beta}\left[v_{i}\mu^{1/2}\right]\right\},\partial^{\alpha}{\{\bf I_{+}-P_{+}\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)-\partial^{\alpha}{\{\bf I_{-}-P_{-}\}}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\rangle\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|\nabla^{|\alpha|}\{{\bf I-P}\}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|_{\nu}^{2}+\left\|\nabla^{|\alpha|+1}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{\nu}+\mathcal{E}_{f^{m,\epsilon},N}(t)\mathcal{D}_{f^{P,\epsilon},N}(t)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}\geq m,\atop 0<j_{1},j_{2}\leq m}\mathcal{E}_{f^{j_{1},\epsilon},N}(t)\mathcal{D}_{f^{j_{2},\epsilon},N}(t)

and

\displaystyle\left(\partial^{\alpha}\left\{q_{1}\left\{E^{m,\epsilon}+\epsilon b^{f^{m,\epsilon}}\times\left\{B^{P}+\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}\epsilon^{k}B^{k}\right\}\right\}\cdot v\mu^{1/2}\right\},w^{2}_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}\partial^{\alpha}f^{m,\epsilon}\right)

(2.61)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4{C_{0}}}\frac{d}{dt}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\left\|\left\langle\left\{w^{2}_{\ell-|\beta|,\kappa}\left[v_{i}\mu^{1/2}\right]\right\},\partial^{\alpha}f_{+}^{m,\epsilon}(t)-\partial^{\alpha}f_{-}^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\rangle\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|\nabla^{|\alpha|}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|_{\nu}^{2}+\left\|\nabla^{|\alpha|+1}f^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{\nu}+\mathcal{E}_{f^{m,\epsilon},N}(t)\mathcal{D}_{f^{P,\epsilon},N}(t)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}\geq m,\atop 0<j_{1},j_{2}\leq m}\mathcal{E}_{f^{j_{1},\epsilon},N}(t)\mathcal{D}_{f^{j_{2},\epsilon},N}(t),

which do not include
\left\|\partial^{\alpha}E^{m,\epsilon}(t)\right\|
any more,
such that the terms in the right hand side of the above inequalities can be absorbed by the corresponding dissipation functionals. Here
\mathcal{E}_{f^{j,\epsilon},N}(t)(j=1,2,\cdots,m),\mathcal{E}_{f^{P,\epsilon},N}(t)
, and
\mathcal{D}_{f^{j,\epsilon},N}(t)(j=1,2,\cdots,m),\mathcal{D}_{f^{P,\epsilon},N}(t)
are defined by (
2.1.2
) and (
2.1.2
), respectively.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Section 2 and Section 3 are devoted to the proofs of Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
2.2
respectively. Some preliminaries and some tedious but standard calculations will be given in Appendix A and Appendix B for brevity.

3.
The proof of Theorem
2.1

In this section, we prove Theorem
2.1
. For this purpose, suppose that
\left[f^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
is a unique solution of the Cauchy problem (
2.3
), (
2.4
), (
2.5
) defined on the strip
\Pi_{T}=[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
for some given positive constant
T>0
, we now turn to deduce some energy type
a priori
estimates on
\left[f^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
in terms of the corresponding initial data
\left[f^{\epsilon}_{0}(x,v),E^{\epsilon}_{0}(x),\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}_{0}(x)\right]
.

To make the presentation easy to follow, we divide this section into several subsections and the first one is on the macro-structure for
\left[f^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]
.

3.1.
Macro-structure for
\left[f^{\epsilon}(t,x,v),E^{\epsilon}(t,x),\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}(t,x)\right]

We first list below some identities concerning the macroscopic part of
f^{\epsilon}(t,x,v)
, which can be obtained by recalling the macro-micro decomposition
(\ref{macro-micro})
introduced in
[
18
]
, multiplying (
2.3
) by some
\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}-
type terms and by integrating the resulting identities with respect to
v
over
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

We can get that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{+}+a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{-}}{2}\right)+\nabla_{x}\cdot b^{f^{\epsilon}}=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}b^{f^{\epsilon}}_{i}+\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{+}+a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{-}}{2}+2c^{f^{\epsilon}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\partial_{j}\mathbb{A}_{ij}(\{{\bf I-P}\}{f^{\epsilon}}\cdot[1,1])=\frac{a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{+}-a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{-}}{2}E^{\epsilon}_{i}+\epsilon\left[G^{\epsilon}\times\mathfrak{B}\right]_{i}+\epsilon\left[G^{\epsilon}\times\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}\right]_{i},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c^{f^{\epsilon}}+\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{x}\cdot b^{f^{\epsilon}}+\frac{5}{6}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{3}\partial_{i}\mathbb{B}_{i}\left(\{{\bf I-P}\}{f^{\epsilon}}\cdot[1,1]\right)=\frac{1}{6}G^{\epsilon}\cdot E^{\epsilon}

(3.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{+}-a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{-}\right)+\nabla_{x}\cdot G^{\epsilon}=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}G^{\epsilon}+\nabla_{x}\left(a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{+}-a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{-}\right)-2E^{\epsilon}+\nabla_{x}\cdot\mathbb{A}\left(\{{\bf I-P}\}{f^{\epsilon}}\cdot q_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=E^{\epsilon}\left(a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{+}+a^{f^{\epsilon}}_{-}\right)+2\epsilon b^{f^{\epsilon}}\times\mathfrak{B}+2\epsilon b^{f^{\epsilon}}\times\widetilde{B}^{\epsilon}+\left\langle[v,-v]\mu^{1/2},L{f^{\epsilon}}+{\Gamma}_{\pm}\left(f^{\epsilon},f^{\epsilon}\right)\right\rangle

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\mathbb{A}_{ij}\left(\{{\bf I-P}\}{f^{\epsilon}}\cdot[1,1]\right)+\partial_{j}b^{f^{\epsilon}}_{i}+\partial_{i}b^{f^{\epsilon}}_{j}-\frac{2}{3}\delta_{ij}\nabla_{x}\cdot b^{f^{\epsilon}}-\frac{5}{3}\delta_{ij}\nabla_{x}\cdot\mathbb{B}\left(\{{\bf I-P}\}{f^{\epsilon}}\cdot[1,1]\right)

(3.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{A}_{ij}\left(r^{\epsilon}_{+}+r^{\epsilon}_{-}+g^{\epsilon}_{+}+g^{\epsilon}_{-}\right)-\frac{1}{3}\delta_{ij}G^{\epsilon}\cdot E^{\epsilon},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}_{j}\left(\{{\bf I-P}\}{f^{\epsilon}}\cdot[1,1]\right)+\partial_{j}c^{f^{\epsilon}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}_{j}\left(r^{\epsilon}_{+}+r^{\epsilon}_{-}+g^{\epsilon}_{+}+g^{\epsilon}_{-}\right),

where

\displaystyle r^{\epsilon}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=
